US Utilities
UBS Research
ANALYSIS OF PRICING POWER IN REGULATED UTILITIES
Given investor focus on inflation and its potential impacts, we analyzed the "pricing 
power" of utilities by calculating annual bill increases. Instead of just a straight analysis 
of annual increases in bills, we took the estimated increase  divided by the weighted 
average personal income by jurisdiction for each company. We then estimated the total 
increase in that metric through 2028 and annualized that number.  We believe this is a 
more accurate representation of "pricing power" for a utility as it takes into account the 
relative affordability of the projected bill increases across a company's jurisdictions.
To calculate bill inflation, we used each company's projected capital plan and extended 
that at flat capex to 2028 from the end of each company's forecast to determine rate 
base growth.  For fuel expenses, we used forward curves and retired all coal generation 
by 2028 and replaced it with a third each of solar, wind, and natural gas.  We 
incorporated non-fuel O&M increases of 2%/year, held interest expense at 4%, used 30-
year depreciation, and an allowed return of 9.85% and a 48% equity ratio.  While 
individual companies may achieve better or worse metrics, we tried to create an average 
metric across companies over the eight-year time frame.  We also used retail sales 
growth of 1% per year.
To calculate disposable income, we took each state or province's disposable income in 
2021 and grew it by forecast gross state (provincial) product through 2023 and then 
held annual GSP growth constant through 2028.  For each company we weighted this by 
earnings power by jurisdiction to determine the weighted average disposable income.
Dividing annual bills by weighted average disposable income projected into 2028 and 
seeing how much that grew versus the same metric in 2021, and  annualizing that 
growth provides a measure of bill affordability inflation.  When we graph this against 
annualized projected utility and parent only EPS growth, the results are shown in Figure 
1 below.  We have also overlayed the weighted average regulatory ranking quartile 
(discussed in the next section) onto this graph through the color of the dots:  1st quartile 
is green, 2nd quartile is blue, 3rd quartile is yellow, and 4th quartile is red. This relative 
regulatory quartile is important, as more constructive regulation will likely allow for more 
EPS growth with  more affordability inflation, all else equal.
In Figure 1, about half of the companies end up around the median level of growth and 
affordability inflation of 6.5% and just under 1%, respectively.  Those outside of the 
median grouping end up in one of four quadrants: (I) high growth and low affordability 
inflation - where there is clear pricing power due to lower cost structures or higher 
disposable income growth; (II) high growth and higher affordability inflation, where 
there is pricing power but it requires constructive regulation or policy support to be 
maintained; (III) lower growth and higher affordability inflation - where there is lower 
pricing power and it would require constructive regulation or, more likely a state or 
provincial energy policy initiative to accelerate growth, and (IV) low growth and low 
affordability inflation - where there is an opportunity to accelerate growth if regulation is 
constructive or where a company might get stuck if regulation is more challenging.
We are Buy-rated on all the names outside the median range in quadrants (I) and (II), 
except SO, as we believe policy initiatives prevent pricing power deterioration in 
quadrant (II).  This is true in our view for PCG - California wildfire mitigation spending, 
EXC - Illinois return to normal vs. formulaic rate cases and multi-year rate plans in other 
jurisdictions; NEE - recently decided rate case in Florida, and PEG - upcoming 
infrastructure advancement spending, and energy strong resiliency and climate 
mitigation spending as well as gas system modernization spending. We believe in the 
longer-term benefits of those companies that show pricing power in quadrant (I), but 
see risks to SO related to the Vogtle new nuclear construction project.  In quadrant 
(IV) we are Buy-rated on PPL and DUK where we see the capability to accelerate growth
once current regulatory processes are complete, but are Sell-rated on PNW due to
Arizona's Tier 5 regulatory ranking.  In quadrant (III) we see the necessary state or
provincial policy to accelerate growth and have a valuation driven bias to
AEP (transmission and regulated renewables), EMA (decarbonization in Nova Scotia),
and D (offshore wind and onshore solar under the Clean Energy Act in Virginia).  We are
Neutral on NI and WEC.

Weighted Average Regulatory Rankings
We also rank the companies in our coverage universe by weighted average regulatory 
ranking, as constructive regulation allows for generally higher levels of capital 
investment and rate base growth, and better, on average, on  cost recovery to translate 
that rate base growth into earnings growth.  Less constructive regulation is more likely, 
all else equal to put pressure on return spreads, be more balanced between customer 
rates and growth potential and reinvestment in the system, and allow less, on average, 
cost recovery which could limit the ability to translate rate base growth into earnings 
growth.
Since our last update we added CNP and NI to our coverage universe, so for quartile 
purposes we now cover 29 regulated utilities vs. 27 previously, but quartiles are still 
generally seven names.  Companies that moved up a quartile are: 2nd to 1st  - EMA and 
LNT; 3rd to 2nd - PEG; and 4th to 3rd - EIX and SRE.  Companies that moved down a 
quartile are: 2nd from 1st - AES and DUK; 3rd from 2nd - PPL; and 4th from 3rd - ETR and 
EVRG.

Valuation Method and Risk Statement
North America Utilities:
Our valuation methodology for the group is price-to-earnings based. The adjustments applied 
fall into 7 categories. These are as follows: 1) Group Valuation Bias: Flowing from our 
valuation work comparing Baa corporate yields to group dividend yields and RU price to 
earnings ratios to those for the S&P 500, we incorporate a positive or negative adjustment to 
our group multiple representing the gap we calculate to the nearest 5%; 2) Growth 
Adjustment: We adjust our valuations based on the growth quartile each utility occupies. First 
quartile receives a 5% premium, second quartile a 2% premium, third quartile a 2% discount 
and fourth quartile a 5% discount; 3) Regulatory Adjustment: Our valuation adjustments for 
regulation are based on our proprietary Regulatory Rankings. First quartile jurisdictions 
receive 5%, second quartile 2%, third quartile -2% and fourth quartile -5%; 4) ESG: A 
potential 5% premium for a risk adjusted clean energy transition growth opportunity; 5) 
Earnings Consistency Adjustment: For companies that fall in the top quartile of % Time Beat/ 
Meet, we include +5%; 6) Multi Utility Diversified Valuation: For multi utilities (those with 
more than 15% of unregulated earnings), we perform a sum-of-the-parts analysis applying 
business/region appropriate valuations to those diversified businesses; 7) One-off 
Adjustments: In special situations, we value risk on an issue-specific basis. Common areas 
where we apply such an adjustment include: large project construction risk, legal risk, and 
announced M&A completion risk. We identify the following risk factors: rising interest rates; 
regulatory and policy risks; operational risks; construction risks; cybersecurity risk to the 
transmission grid and/or customer data, and extreme weather events.
Water Utilities:
We value water utilities on a price-to-earnings basis on our 2023 estimates. Our valuation 
methodology is premised upon a group valuation bias, forecast 2021-2025 earnings growth, 
our proprietary analysis of regulatory jurisdictional rankings, exposure to states with fair value 
legislation, and in some cases a company-specific adjustment. We identify the following risk 
factors: rising interest rate; regulatory and policy risks; operational risks; construction risks; 
water quality, safety and contamination risk; physical risk to the water sources, treatment or 
delivery systems; cybersecurity risk; drought and extreme weather events.
Power:
We use an EV/EBITDA based methodology and a 7.1x FY2 mid-cycle multiple. We identify the 
following risk factors: power prices, weather, retail margins, natural gas prices, electricity 
supply and demand dynamics, and nuclear operational risk.

KEY DEFINITIONS: Forecast Stock Return (FSR) is defined as expected percentage price appreciation plus gross dividend yield over 
the next 12 months. In some cases, this yield may be based on accrued dividends. Market Return Assumption (MRA) is defined as 
the one-year local market interest rate plus 5% (a proxy for, and not a forecast of, the equity risk premium). Under Review (UR) 
Stocks may be flagged as UR by the analyst, indicating that the stock's price target and/or rating are subject to possible change in the 
near term, usually in response to an event that may affect the investment case or valuation. Short-Term Ratings reflect the expected 
near-term (up to three months) performance of the stock and do not reflect any change in the fundamental view or investment case. 
Equity Price Targets have an investment horizon of 12 months.

such as structure, management, performance record, discount; Neutral: Neutral on factors such as structure, management, 
performance record, discount; Sell: Negative on factors such as structure, management, performance record, discount. Core 
Banding Exceptions (CBE): Exceptions to the standard +/-6% bands may be granted by the Investment Review Committee (IRC). 
Factors considered by the IRC include the stock's volatility and the credit spread of the respective company's debt. As a result, stocks 
deemed to be very high or low risk may be subject to higher or lower bands as they relate to the rating. When such exceptions apply, 
they will be identified in the Company Disclosures table in the relevant research piece.

Investing in structured investments involves significant risks. For a detailed discussion of the risks involved in investing in any particular structured investment, you must read 
the relevant offering materials for that investment. Structured investments are unsecured obligations of a particular issuer with returns linked to the performance of an 
underlying asset. Depending on the terms of the investment, investors could lose all or a substantial portion of their investment based on the performance of the underlying 
asset. Investors could also lose their entire investment if the issuer becomes insolvent. UBS Financial Services Inc. does not guarantee in any way the obligations or the 
financial condition of any issuer or the accuracy of any financial information provided by any issuer. Structured investments are not traditional investments and investing in a 
structured investment is not equivalent to investing directly in the underlying asset. Structured investments may have limited or no liquidity, and investors should be prepared 
to hold their investment to maturity. The return of structured investments may be limited by a maximum gain, participation rate or other feature. Structured investments may 
include call features and, if a structured investment is called early, investors would not earn any further return and may not be able to reinvest in similar investments with 
similar terms. Structured investments include costs and fees which are generally embedded in the price of the investment. The tax treatment of a structured investment may 
be complex and may differ from a direct investment in the underlying asset. UBS Financial Services Inc. and its employees do not provide tax advice. Investors should consult 
their own tax advisor about their own tax situation before investing in any securities.

otherwise would be consistent with its investment objective and other principal investment strategies. The returns on a portfolio consisting primarily of sustainable

investing issues; there is also no guarantee that any company will meet expectations in connection with corporate responsibility, sustainability, and/or impact performance.

   Equity Research
Rating Change — May 2, 2022
Regulated Electric Utilities
Rating Actions: Downgrade ED to Underweight & Upgrade IDA to Equal
Weight
Our Call
The utility group has enjoyed a nice run since late ’21 with the S&P Utilities outperforming the
S&P 500 by 17% since 11/30. During the same time period, ED has outperformed and IDA
has underperformed (see Exhibit 1). We move from Equal Weight to Underweight on shares of
ED as we are not yet ready to apply an in-line multiple (where shares currently trade) on the
NY regulatory/political landscape. On the ip side, we upgrade shares of IDA to Equal Weight
from Underweight as the premium P/E multiple has compressed to what we consider to be a
reasonable level.

ED to Underweight. Over the past year, shares of ED are +24% vs. +10% for Regulated
Electric peers. With the move, the stock now trades at an in-line P/E multiple, which
compares with the 3- and 5-year median discount of 11% & 6%, respectively. We attribute
the move to an improved growth outlook (targeting 5-7%), positive regulatory data
points (including the O&R settlement) and strategic developments (Stagecoach sale and
plans to further streamline via a strategic review of the Clean Energy Businesses). That
said, we think ED has a tricky road to navigate in NY given a large rate request pending
with a backdrop of rising commodity prices causing customer bill pressure. While we
believe ConEd makes a strong case in terms of investments needed to execute on de-
carbonization goals, we think NY could be more susceptible to populist pressures than
other states. As such, we apply a ~5% discount in our valuation and we downgrade shares
to Underweight from Equal Weight. We increase our price target to $94/sh (19.4X our
‘23E EPS of $4.85) from $85/sh, reecting meaningfully higher peer group multiples
since our last update.
For investors looking for defensive appeal in the Regulated Electric Utility space, we favor
shares of AEE (quality) and ETR (value).
IDA to Equal Weight. Shares of IDA have lagged All Cap Regulated Electric peers by 15%
over the past 5 months. While the company has announced a healthy increase in capex,
which we view favorably, that also brings Idaho Power Company (IPC) closer to a base rate

embeds a 5-10% premium to All Cap peers.

Regulated Electric Utilities
Equity Research
Bull/Bear Scenarios:
ED: Upside/Downside to our $94/sh price target, which applies a ~5% P/E discount to Mid/Large
Cap Regulated Electric peers on our ‘23E of $4.85:
In an upside case, CECONY secures a better-than-expected rate case outcome allowing the company
to shore up under-earnings and reecting a 9.3% allowed ROE (vs. current allowed 8.8% and O&R’s
9.2%). In this scenario, we see ‘23E EPS power of $5.05, which is 4% above our estimate. Applying an
in-line multiple (which reects a more constructive regulatory view vs. our base case ~5% discount),
supports a $103/sh price target.
In a bear case, the PSC pushes back strongly on ED’s proposed capex plan and does not approve a rate
increase that allows CECONY to earn near the allowed ROE. Our downside case supports a ‘23E EPS of
$4.80 and, more impactful, envisions ED reverting to a ~10% P/E discount, which results in a forward
price target of $88/sh.
IDA: Upside/Downside to our $110/sh price target, which applies a 5-10% P/E premium to All Cap
Regulated Electric peers on our ‘23E of $5.20:
In an upside case, IDA continues to grow EPS at a 5% clip (above our 3-4% forecast), which is supported
by a very constructive rate case outcome for Idaho Power (IPC). This represents 5% upside to our ‘24E
EPS and supports a price target of $114-116/sh (high end of our 5-10% premium range).
In a bear case, IPC’s rate case proceeding proves challenging and results in an allowed ROE of 9.5% or
below (vs. our assumed 9.75%), which supports a ’24E EPS is in a range of $5.00-5.15, 4-5% below our
estimate. The perception of the Idaho regulatory environment worsens causing the premium multiple
to further compress. Under this scenario, we see a forward price target of $103-105/sh.

Regulated Electric Utilities
Equity Research
Investment Thesis, Valuation and Risks
Ameren Corporation (AEE)
Investment Thesis
Our Overweight thesis reects AEE's strong 5-yr annual EPS growth prospects of 7.5% (vs. 6% for peers) and management's strong
nanical track record.
Target Price Valuation for AEE: $100.00
Our $100/sh price target is based on of (1) a P/E analysis ($100/sh) - apply a 15% premium to the '22 Regulated Electric median of ~20X
on our 23E EPS of $4.35 and (2) a three-stage dividend discount analysis ($101/sh), which employs a 6.5-7.0% discount rate.
Risks to Our Price Target and Rating for AEE
Risks include the potential failure to achieve earned returns near the utilities' allowed ROEs, regulatory related risks and the risk that upside
capex opportunities do not materialize as expected.
 
Consolidated Edison, Inc. (ED)
Investment Thesis
We are attracted to ED's infrastructure-driven business model with upside opportunities related to renewable development and
renewable-enabling T&D projects. Our Equal Weight rating reects valuation considerations.
Target Price Valuation for ED: $94.00 from $85.00
Our $94/sh price target is primarily based on our P/E multiple analysis (19.4X our '23E EPS - reects a ~5% discount to the 2022 Mid/
Large Cap Regulated Electric median of ~19.5X).
Risks to Our Price Target and Rating for ED
Risks to our Underweight rating include new growth projects at the electric transmission (CET) segment, a better-than-expected outcome
to the pending ConEd rate case and a value-enhancing outcome to the strategic review of the clean energy segment (CEB) (we assume a
transaction would be neutral to EPS).
 
Entergy Corp. (ETR)
Investment Thesis
Our Overweight rating reects our assessment that the current share price does not adequately reect ETR's strong EPS growth outlook
(driven by regulated infrastructure investment) and above-average regulatory frameworks.
Target Price Valuation for ETR: $135.00
Our $135/sh price target is based on a P/E multiple analysis and is also supported by our DDM analysis. (1) P/E Multiple ($135/sh)—we
apply a 5% discount to the '22 Mid/Large Cap Regulated P/E multiple of ~21x on our 23E EPS of $6.75. (2) DDM Analysis ($138/sh)—key
assumptions include 10-yr EPS and dividend per share growth of 4.5-5.0%, long-term sustainable growth of 3% and a 6.75% discount rate.
Risks to Our Price Target and Rating for ETR
Risks to our target include possible adverse regulatory outcomes (most notably SERI) and elevated storm damage risks (cost recovery)
related to hurricanes due to ETR's Gulf Coast footprint.
 
IDACORP, Inc. (IDA)
Investment Thesis
We are attracted to IDA's constructive regulatory treatment, execution track record, and growing service territory. Our Equal Weight rating
reects valuation considerations in concert with our forecasted below-aveage EPS growth rate.
Target Price Valuation for IDA: $110.00 from $108.00
Our $110/share price target is primarily based on a P/E multiple analysis (apply a 21X multiple, a 5-10% premium to the All Cap Regulated
Electric median of ~20X to our '23E EPS).
Risks to Our Price Target and Rating for IDA
Downside risks include a subpar outcome in the upcoming Idaho Power rate case, a slowdown in sales growth and an inability to manage
inationary pressures on costs. Upside risks include a continued acceleration in customer growth including new large load customers,
better-than-expected cost controls, and a pick-up in M&A activity, which could increase the takeout premium in small cap utilities.

STOCK RATING
1=Overweight: Total return on stock expected to be 10%+ over the next 12 months. BUY
2=Equal Weight: Total return on stock expected to be -10% to +10% over the next 12 months. HOLD
3=Underweight: Total return on stock expected to lag the Overweight- and Equal Weight-rated stocks within the analyst's coverage universe over the next 12
months. SELL
As of May 1, 2022
55.9% of companies covered by Wells Fargo Securities, LLC Equity Research are rated Overweight.
37.0% of companies covered by Wells Fargo Securities, LLC Equity Research are rated Equal Weight.
7.1% of companies covered by Wells Fargo Securities, LLC Equity Research are rated Underweight.
Wells Fargo Securities, LLC has provided investment banking services for 50.9% of its Equity Research Overweight-rated companies.
Wells Fargo Securities, LLC has provided investment banking services for 45.0% of its Equity Research Equal Weight-rated companies.
Wells Fargo Securities, LLC has provided investment banking services for 33.8% of its Equity Research Underweight-rated companies.

This report was prepared by Wells Fargo Securities Global Research Department (“WFS Research”) personnel associated with Wells Fargo Securities, LLC ("Wells
Fargo Securities").
WFS Research may, from time to time, provide clients with short-term trading views in its research reports regarding subject companies on which Wells Fargo
Securities currently has equity research coverage. A short-term trading view oers a view on how the market price of a subject company’s common equity may
trend in absolute terms during the 30 days following the date of the short-term trading view. A short-term trading view on a subject company’s common equity
does not impact our fundamental investment rating or price target for that company, which reect our view of how the subject company’s common equity may
perform over a one-year period. A short-term trading view may reach a dierent conclusion than the rm’s fundamental investment rating and price target for
a subject company and, therefore, short-term trading views could result in short-term price movements that are contrary to our fundamental investment rating
and price target. Short-term trading views are not ratings and the rm does not intend, nor undertakes any obligation, to maintain, update or close out short-term
trading views. Short-term trading views may not be suitable for all investors and have not been tailored to individual investor circumstances and objectives, and
investors should make their own independent decisions regarding any short-term trading views discussed in WFS Research reports.

U.S. Utilities and Power
1Q22 Earnings Roundup: Week Three
OUR TAKE: Slightly Positive. We provide a recap of last week's earnings for the companies
under our coverage. Most companies met or beat consensus earnings expectations; WEC’s
beat stands out in particular, and while CNP and ED were each short of consensus, each
only missed by a penny. Most of the calls focused on price and schedule risks for some solar
projects, managing inflation, and de-risking company portfolios.
WEC Energy Group, Inc. (Sector Outperform) – They Must Be DJs With All These Beats:
Positive. Another year, another beat-and-raise. We remain bullish on WEC as we believe the
company is among the highest quality in the industry; we’re drawn to the consistent, above-
average growth and the favorable regulatory/political environments, particularly in WI, the
company’s primary state. The company’s track record around earnings beats is stellar: WEC
has met or beaten the midpoints of original guidance ranges in each of the past 18 years,
and has exceeded the high end in nine of the past 10 years. With a strong 1Q22 beat-and-
raise, the company seems well-positioned to extend both streaks. We increase our 2022 EPS
estimate and remain confident in our Street-high forecasts for all years. While the recently-
filed WI rate case may bring some headline or regulatory risks, we see no reason to worry.
Lastly, while management acknowledges price and schedule risks for some solar and battery
projects, we’re encouraged by the company’s confident tone (2023 spending on solar and
batteries is <3% of the total WEC five-year capex plan and WEC stresses its flexibility). WEC
reported 1Q22 EPS of $1.79, crushing our/consensus estimates of $1.68/$1.69 and guidance of
$1.68-$1.70. Link to our earnings note.
CenterPoint Energy, Inc. (Sector Perform) – Another Update, Another Capex Increase:
Slightly Positive. We remain impressed with CNP’s execution and are upbeat about the
outlook. The biggest news from the 1Q22 update, in our view, was yet another increase to the
capital plan, including some shifting around of the timing of certain projects. Management
now forecasts $19.3B of capex over the coming five years, up from $19.2B most recently (this
was the second increase since the September 2021 Analyst Day). Within the period, CNP
now forecasts $4.3B of capex in 2022, up from $4.0B, reflecting ~$100m of incremental
routine work (part of the ~$1B of “reserve capital”) and an acceleration of ~$200m from
future years, primarily 2023. CNP pulled that spending forward to de-risk/offset potential
adverse financial impacts that could come from solar delays given widely-known supply chain
issues. For perspective, the next solar project isn’t scheduled until 4Q23, and management
estimates <$0.02/sh of earnings in 2024 from the generation transition. While management
isn’t making changes to the capex plan due to inflationary pressures per se, the company
is “clearly… starting to feel the impacts…” and views inflation not as a “driver” but rather
a “potential risk”. Longer-term, management is sticking with the 10-year, $40B+ outlook
for now, but seems confident that the company will identify incremental capex over time –
particularly related to the Resilient Now collaboration with Houston; we expect updates on
that front later this year as the initiative progresses, likely around 3Q22 earnings season/
EEI. Beyond capex, fundamentals look attractive. The regulatory calendar is blissfully quiet,
with no major rate cases until late 2023 following the recent MN gas rate case settlement.
While affordability is always a concern, and likely now more than ever, CNP is uniquely well-
positioned to benefit from robust demand growth (11 consecutive years of 2%+ customer
growth in Houston), the customer benefits of 1%-2% annual O&M reductions, and the roll-offs
of securitization charges related to 2007’s Hurricane Ike in August (charges contribute ~3% to
current residential bills) and related to the 1999 electric market restructuring in 2024 (~5% of
current bills). These savings will help offset the bill impacts of the capital spending program.
Lastly, we credit management for successfully exiting midstream faster than we had expected,
and with notably larger net proceeds ($1.3B) than we had anticipated thanks to the run-up
in midstream sector valuations lately. The proceeds reinforce the company’s lack of equity
needs, as would cash inflows related to the evaluation of the company’s repairs expense
deduction methodology, which could generate ~$300m in 2022 and another ~$100m over
the subsequent four years, according to management’s estimates (up $50m from CNP’s
This report is intended for leejar@coned.com. Unauthorized distribution of this report is prohibited.

 prior estimate). Still, we remain on the sidelines as we see the modest P/E premium vs. the
median of our coverage as reasonable given robust growth and a quiet near-term regulatory
outlook mitigated by sometimes-challenging regulatory environments and a limited track
record as a pure-play utility. CNP reported 1Q22 EPS of $0.47, beating our estimate of $0.46
but shy of consensus of $0.48.
CMS Energy Corporation (Sector Outperform) – Investing to Reduce Pollution (IRP):
Positive. With a strong 1Q22 beat, we see CMS as well-positioned to deliver on FY22 results.
Though the regulatory calendar is busy with electric and gas rate cases underway, we
view the IRP settlement quite favorably. We believe the deal reinforces the strength of
the MI environment, both in terms of regulation and relationships with key interveners.
Importantly, the IRP improves visibility into the sustainability of long-term generation capex
while solidifying the company’s decarbonization plan (including a full exit from coal by
2025). We remain bullish on CMS, which remains among our top picks and is our favorite for
defensiveness; we believe that the stock should be particularly attractive to investors looking
to hedge their portfolios with a stable, low-beta offset to riskier holdings. CMS reported 1Q22
EPS of $1.20, well ahead of our/consensus forecasts of $1.14/$1.13. Link to our earnings note.
Public Service Enterprise Group Incorporated (Sector Perform) – Strong Operations, but
an Uncertain Outlook for the Non-Utility Business: Positive. We remain impressed with
PSEG’s simplified and de-risked story following the sales of merchant power generation,
the implementation of revenue decoupling (CIP), and various regulatory approvals for
capex. We continue to see the growth outlook as robust, driven by capex as well as steady
account growth. While the 1Q22 beat was certainly impressive, some of the positive y/y
drivers seem likely to reverse over the year (management reiterated the 2022 EPS guidance
range, not surprisingly). The company is currently engaged in settlement talks regarding
$848m of capex over four years related to the NJ Infrastructure Advancement Program;
based on the state’s history, we see a settlement as probable, but expect it will likely cover
fewer dollars spread over more years. PEG expects final regulatory approval by the fall.
Regarding customer affordability, we appreciate the smoothing impact of how PEG, as a
NJ utility, procures electricity and gas from its deregulated markets, mitigating the impacts
of recent commodity price spikes. The company’s focus on payment assistance programs
also helps, particularly as shutoff moratoriums are being lifted. Regarding the unregulated
nukes, while we share management’s view that sentiment toward nuclear power plants has
improved lately, particularly since the Russian invasion and subsequent concerns about
energy independence, we’re less upbeat about long-term policy support. PEG’s plants won’t
qualify for the first round of DOE funding as state support makes them not at risk of near-
term early retirement, but we expect the company and its partners to apply in subsequent
rounds. Still, management doesn’t see these efforts from the DOE as being sufficient to justify
long-term investment decisions, including around fuel purchases and license extensions.
While media reports indicate that Senator Manchin and other Senators are attempting to
pass a more-focused clean energy legislation, unfortunately, we aren’t hopeful that a deal
will get done with sufficient time before midterm elections this fall. We therefore continue
to question the long-term value of the plants, including their ultimate ownership (we expect
PEG to sell in 12-24 months regardless of policy outcomes, though management remains non-
committal). We do see potential for a long-term solution from NJ, but details are uncertain
and such a program would add to customer bills. If nothing else, though, the spike in natural
gas and power forwards bodes well for the nukes’ earnings power in the outer years; we
expect positive financial updates with the upcoming investor conference in September. On
offshore wind, management has identified $1-$3B of transmission capex opportunities related
to the Coastal Wind Link partnership with Ørsted, up from a prior estimate of $1B. We also
anticipate that the partnership will continue to bid for future capacity solicitations, but expect
heightened competition to lead to returns that might not meet the company’s thresholds.
PEG’s balance sheet looks quite strong following cash proceeds from the Power asset sales,
including most recently, $1.9B of pre-tax proceeds from the Fossil sale; management expects
to complete the $500m repurchase program by mid-year. Even if capex forecasts increase,
PEG seems committed to not issuing new equity. Lastly, regarding the “Ralph swap” CEO

 transition, we remain confident that there won’t be any noteworthy changes in the corporate
strategy, key relationships, or track record of execution. Overall, we like PEG’s refocused and
de-risked growth outlook, but see the modest P/E discount to the median of our coverage as
fair given the average pace of EPS growth and LT risks to the merchant nukes and offshore
wind. PEG reported 1Q22 EPS of $1.33, crushing our/consensus estimates of $1.18/$1.13.
Eversource Energy (Sector Perform) – We Like to Stay in the Shallow Water: Positive.
We welcome ES’s evaluation of its offshore wind interest and believe that a sale would
likely be accretive to earnings and the stock’s valuation. While some investors are drawn
to the contribution to the company’s ESG profile, we’ve instead long worried about the
merchant nature of the projects and the nascency of the U.S. offshore wind industry. Risks are
dramatically lower than they were a few years ago, but given the industry’s ongoing aggressive
transition toward being more plain-vanilla, we expect that redeploying capital from offshore
wind to rate-based projects will improve the quality of the company’s earnings. Given the
potential for an attractive sale, we increase our TP by a buck, to $94, though a lot of work will
need to be done to announce and close a sale. Beyond that, we continue to view ES as a high-
quality, defensive utility company and stock, though we see better opportunities for faster
risk-adjusted growth at comparable or cheaper valuations elsewhere. ES reported 1Q22 EPS
of $1.30, well ahead of our and consensus estimates of $1.21 and $1.19, respectively. Link to our
earnings note.
Dominion Energy, Inc. (Sector Outperform) – Dominion’s Story Is as Clean as the Energy:
Slightly Positive. D continues to execute, both in terms of operations, delivering on earnings
expectations, and actively de-risking the outlook. We see the current ~5% P/E discount to the
median of our coverage as highly attractive and instead argue for a relative premium. We’re
drawn to the precision and consistency of EPS guidance and growth, the pace of which should
be only slightly above average but with a narrow range of potential outcomes, as well as the
below-average regulatory risks. D reported 1Q22 EPS of $1.18, a penny short of our estimate
but a penny better than consensus and perfectly in line with the midpoint of the quarterly
guidance range. Link to our earnings note.
Black Hills Corporation (Sector Outperform) – Stability Today, Upside Tomorrow: Positive.
With a strong 1Q22 result, we see BKH as well-positioned to deliver or exceed earnings
expectations for FY22. For now, we maintain our $4.05 EPS estimate, which matches the
midpoint of the guidance range, but we recognize upside potential; we’re the Street-low,
we believe. We credit management for delicately balancing capex needs with concerns
around the balance sheet and customer affordability in today’s inflationary environment. The
company plans to maintain spending levels planned for 2022-2023, but given upward cost
pressures, will consider delaying certain non-essential projects beyond 2023 in order to not
exceed them. This in turn should create potential for substantial positive revisions to capex
and thus earnings estimates in 2024+, while keeping financials and customer bill impacts
stable in the interim. We see the stock’s ~10% P/E discount as being very attractive given the
outlook for peer-average EPS/DPS growth of ~6% per year, with upside from incremental
capital opportunities. BKH reported 1Q22 EPS of $1.82, well ahead of our consensus-matching
estimate of $1.65. Link to our earnings note.
Consolidated Edison, Inc. (Sector Underperform) – The Regulatory Back-and-Forth Begins:
Neutral or Slightly Negative. The focus for ED through all of 2022 will be on the CECONY
rate case, in our view. While ED’s financial and strategic requests all seem reasonable and
productive, we worry about the extremely challenging regulatory and political environment
in NY. We expect pushback against certain capital and O&M requests, and worry about
heightened affordability concerns given inflationary pressures, particularly around commodity
costs. The PSC already requested that ED enhance its efforts to reduce bill volatility following
pushback from politicians (and the media), and ED has proposed adjusting the company’s
procurement, hedging, and communications strategies. In our view, these types of back-and-
forths reinforce our bearishness on NY – every utility in the country is facing inflation, yet ED
seems to be getting more backlash than any other, even despite operating in a deregulated

 market where the company has minimal control over electric and gas pricing. We expect a
final rate case order in December, likely through a settlement as has been the precedent;
settlement hearings will begin in early July. We note that, despite historically challenging
headlines, final rate case outcomes have generally been reasonably constructive for ED. We’re
encouraged by the recent settlement for the O&R rate case (approved in April), also in NY, but
given the small size of that subsidiary, we expect interveners to be more active in the CECONY
case. Beyond the rate case, ED made two adjustments to the financial outlook which should
both impact customers but be neutral to earnings and cash flows. First, management added
$234m of “other” O&M expenses for 2022, primarily related to various surcharges (neutral
to earnings, but will add to customer bill pressure). Mitigating the customer impact, ED
successfully petitioned for NYC to reduce property taxes related to natural gas infrastructure
assets given the expectation that their utilization will fall over time, reducing the economic
value and thus the taxable base (neutral to earnings, but will provide customers some
relief). As a result of this change, the forecasted 2024E rate base was reduced by 1.6%; this
drove management to lower the forecasted three-year rate base CAGR to 7.1% (from 7.6%
previously). Outside of the utilities, we continue to expect ED to successfully monetize its
contracted renewables business at an attractive valuation given the robust demand for these
assets, including from private investors which can lever up more than an IOU could. We expect
that a sale would far more than offset planned equity issuances of $1.6B over 2022-2024, and
then some; we estimate potential proceeds of ~$6.5B-$7.0B (~13x forward EBITDA of ~$500-
$550m). The company hasn’t provided any updates since announcing the consideration of
strategic alternatives with 4Q21 results. Overall, we remain bearish on ED as we believe that
the current modest P/E premium should instead be a relative discount; we see above-average
headline, regulatory, and political risk, compared to a base-case/guidance outlook that calls
for average EPS/DPS growth at best. ED reported 1Q22 EPS of $1.47, missing consensus by a
penny but beating our estimate of $1.45.

CenterPoint Energy, Inc. (CNP-N; US$30.94)

WEC Energy Group, Inc. (WEC-N; US$101.07)

  Definition of Scotiabank, Global Banking and Markets Equity Research Ratings
Scotiabank has a three-tiered rating system, with ratings of Sector Outperform, Sector Perform, and Sector Underperform. Each Research
Analyst assigns a rating that is relative to his or her coverage universe or an index identified by the Research Analyst that includes, but is not
limited to, stocks covered by the Research Analyst.
The rating assigned to each security covered in this report is based on the Scotiabank, Global Banking and Markets Research Analyst’s 12-
month view on the security. Research Analysts may sometimes express in research reports shorter-term views on these securities that may
impact the price of the equity security in a manner directly counter to the Research Analyst’s 12-month view. These shorter-term views
are based upon catalysts or events that may have a shorter-term impact on the market price of the equity securities discussed in research
reports, including but not limited to the inherent volatility of the marketplace. Any such shorter-term views expressed in research report are
distinct from and do not affect the Research Analyst’s 12-month view and are clearly noted as such.
Ratings
 
 
 
Sector Outperform (SO)
The stock is expected to outperform the average 12-month total
return of the analyst’s coverage universe or an index identified by
the analyst that includes, but is not limited to, stocks covered by the
analyst.
Sector Perform (SP)
The stock is expected to perform approximately in line with the
average 12-month total return of the analyst’s coverage universe or
an index identified by the analyst that includes, but is not limited to,
stocks covered by the analyst.
Sector Underperform (SU)
The stock is expected to underperform the average 12-month total
return of the analyst’s coverage universe or an index identified by
the analyst that includes, but is not limited to, stocks covered by the
analyst.
Focus Stock (FS)
As of April 29, 2019, Scotiabank GBM discontinued the Focus
Stock rating. A stock assigned this rating represented an analyst’s
best idea(s); stocks in this category were expected to significantly
outperform the average 12-month total return of the analyst’s
coverage universe or an index identified by the analyst that
included, but was not limited to, stocks covered by the analyst.
Other Ratings
Under Review – The rating has been temporarily placed under
review, until sufficient information has been received and assessed
by the analyst.
Tender – As of January 25, 2021, Scotiabank GBM discontinued the
Tender rating.
Risk Ranking
The Speculative risk ranking reflects exceptionally high financial
and/or operational risk, exceptionally low predictability of financial
results, and exceptionally high stock volatility. The Director of
Research and the Supervisory Analyst jointly make the final
determination of the Speculative risk ranking.

For the purposes of the ratings distribution disclosure FINRA requires members who use a ratings system with terms different than
“buy,” “hold/neutral” and “sell,” to equate their own ratings into these categories. Our Sector Outperform, Sector Perform, and Sector
Underperform ratings are based on the criteria above, but for this purpose could be equated to buy, neutral and sell ratings, respectively.

RRA REGULATORY FOCUS
NY commission staff recommends below-industry-average ROE for CECONY
Monday, May 23, 2022 3:05 PM ET 
By Lisa Fontanella 
Market Intelligence
The staff of the New York Public Service Commission filed testimony in Consolidated Edison Co. of New York Inc.'s pending electric, Case 22-E-0064, and gas, 
Case 22-G-0065, rate cases. The staff recommends that the PSC authorize the company $299.5 million electric and $165.4 million gas rate increases. 
The staff's recommended rate changes are premised upon an 8.80% return on equity (48% of capital) and a 6.45% return on average rate bases valued at 
$25.991 billion for its electric operations and $9.648 billion for its gas operations for a Dec. 31, 2023, test period. 
The staff recommends an electric base rate increase of about $300 million, or roughly $738 million less than the amount sought by the company, or CECONY, 
and a gas rate increase of about $165 million, or about $237 million less than that sought by CECONY. The bulk of the disallowances are related to capital 
structure and the staff's support for a lower ROE than that proposed by CECONY. The staff recommends an 8.80% ROE and a 48% equity ratio compared to 
CECONY's recommendation of a 10% ROE and a 50% equity ratio. If adopted, the 8.8% ROE recommended by staff would be below prevailing nationwide 
industry average equity return authorizations. The staff proposes only modest adjustments to rate base, but more substantial adjustments to net operating 
income items including property taxes and depreciation. CECONY has a strong history of securing multiyear settlements and such deals have been an integral 
part of the rate case process in New York. 
The staff's recommended ROE is below the prevailing electric and gas industry average authorizations nationwide for similar utilities, according to Regulatory 
Research Associates, a group within S&P Global Commodity Insights. 
The average ROE authorized electric utilities was 9.36% in rate cases decided in the 12 months ended March 31, slightly below the 9.38% average for full year 
2021. The average ROE approved for electric distribution utilities, like CECONY, in cases decided during the 12 months ended March 31 was 9.04%, the same 
as that observed for cases decided during full year 2021. 
The average ROE authorized gas utilities was 9.50% in the rate cases decided in the 12 months ended March 31, below the 9.56% average for full year 2021. 
For a discussion of trends in ROE authorizations and other rate case parameters, refer to RRA's Major Rate Case Decisions Quarterly Update. 
The staff's proposed ROE of 8.80% was calculated in accordance with the commission's long-standing policy that relies on a combination of the discounted 
cash flow approach and the capital asset pricing model to set the authorized ROE, with a weighting of two-thirds discounted cash flow and one-third capital 
asset pricing model. 
A final decision in these proceedings is expected in December, with new rates to become effective in January 2023. 
CECONY is a subsidiary of Consolidated Edison Inc. 
Company rate filing  
On Jan. 28, CECONY sought to implement $1.2 billion electric and $502.7 million gas rate increases. The rate increases were premised upon a 10% return on 
equity (50% of capital) and a 7.10% overall return. The filings reflected average rate bases valued at $26.286 billion for its electric operations and $10.030 
billion for its gas operations for a Dec. 31, 2023, test period. CECONY indicated that it intended to explore the negotiation of a multiyear rate plan. Toward that 
end, the company, for settlement purposes, outlined second- and third-year rate increases. 
CECONY indicated that while its cost-of-capital witness recommends an ROE range of 10% to 10.5%, it is seeking a 10.0% ROE "in order to facilitate the 
resolution of the issues in these proceedings." CECONY noted that this return is "conservative" and with the potential for inflation changes going forward, "it is 
important to ensure the ROE set in this case considers the likely future cost of equity." 
CECONY said the primary drivers of its electric rate request include new infrastructure investments related to resiliency, clean energy and gas safety; an 
increase in the proposed ROE to 10.0% as compared to the currently authorized ROE of 8.8%; property taxes; changes to its sales revenue forecast without a 
bill impact because these costs have already been incorporated into bills through the existing revenue decoupling mechanism; and amortization of net deferred 
credits and costs. 
The clean energy proposal includes 200 MW of new solar generation to fund low-income discounts and four battery-storage system projects to enhance 
reliability, resilience and operational flexibility and to serve disadvantaged communities. Current commission policy prohibits the utility from building and owning 
generation. However, CECONY indicates that it should be allowed to have a role in achieving the state's goals under the Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act. Signed into law July 18, 2019, the act establishes a framework for reducing greenhouse gas emissions and increasing renewable electricity 
generation and energy efficiency. 
CECONY sought to continue all deferral accounting and reconciliation mechanisms in effect. These mechanisms include pension and other post-employment 
benefits expense, nonofficer management variable pay, property taxes, municipal infrastructure support costs and environmental site investigation and 
remediation.

CECONY proposed to continue its electric and gas earnings adjustment mechanisms that encourage it to achieve various targets tied to energy efficiency and 
other incentives related to heat pumps, electric vehicles and distributed energy resources in furtherance of the state's Climate Leadership and Community 
Protection Act goals. 
In accordance with the act, the commission is required to consider whether its decisions are "inconsistent with or will interfere with" attaining statewide 
greenhouse gas emissions limits and requires that the commission find that its decisions will not disproportionately burden disadvantaged communities. 
CECONY proposed to implement an inflation reconciliation that would permit the utility to defer the effects of inflation on certain nonlabor expenses. 
The company proposed to continue its existing revenue decoupling mechanisms for its electric and gas operations. 
Based on an updated filing tendered April 8, CECONY is now supporting a $1.038 billion electric rate increase and a $402.2 million gas rate increase. The 
filings reflect a 10% return on equity (50% of capital) and a 7.14% overall return. The filings reflect average rate bases valued at $26.408 billion for its electric 
operations and $9.697 billion for its gas operations for a Dec. 31, 2023, test period. 
The updated filing reflected corrections and updates to the sales forecast, operations and maintenance expenses, taxes, regulatory amortizations, 
depreciation, rate base and cost of capital. 
CECONY intends to explore the negotiation of a multiyear rate plan, and the company outlined revised second- and third-year rate increases. Specifically, the 
company proposed $744.1 million electric and $205.1 million gas rate increases to be effective Jan. 1, 2024, premised upon a 10% return on equity (50% of 
capital) and a 7.16% return on average rate bases valued at $28.762 billion (electric) and $10.506 billion (gas) for a test year ending Dec. 31, 2024. The 
company proposed $614.9 million electric and $176.1 million gas rate increases to be effective Jan. 1, 2025, premised upon a 10% return on equity (50% of 
capital) and a 7.20% return on average rate bases valued at $30.786 billion, for electric and $11.184 billion for gas for a test year ending Dec. 31, 2025. 
The commission has a long history of adopting settlements containing multifaceted multiyear rate plans. Most major utilities are operating under plans that 
include earnings-sharing provisions, with earnings in excess of an established ROE cap to be shared by shareholders and ratepayers, as well as the potential 
for penalties related to service quality and customer service. Additionally, the plans generally include expense reconciliation mechanisms that allow the utilities 
to defer increases in certain expenses, so long as the company's earned ROE remains below specified thresholds. 
CECONY seeks to implement an inflation reconciliation that would permit the utility to defer the effects of inflation on certain nonlabor operations and 
maintenance expenses. 
In addition, CECONY is proposing a new reconciliation mechanism to address wage inflation. "The United States is in the worst bout of inflation in decades," 
said CECONY, noting that "without this reconciliation, simply paying competitive wages could put enough pressure on the company's [operations and 
maintenance] to unduly impact its projects and programs." 
CECONY proposed a threshold for both labor and nonlabor inflation reconciliations at 160 basis points above the GDP deflator inflation rate used to develop 
rates for a specific rate year. CECONY proposed the recovery of incremental costs via deferral if $5 million or under, or surcharge if over $5 million. 
Staff testimony 
RRA calculates that about $260 million of the $738 million difference between the roughly $1,037.8 million electric rate increase supported by CECONY and 
the $299.5 million increase recommended by staff flows the staff's lower rate of return recommendation. Staff-proposed rate base adjustments would lower the 
revenue requirement by $43 million, while suggested disallowances to net operating income, or NOI, account for the remaining $435 million revenue 
requirement difference. 
The major electric NOI adjustments are related to property taxes, $102 million; labor, $77 million; depreciation, $51 million; information technology, $50 million; 
amortization of regulatory deferrals, $43 million; and pension and other post-employment benefits costs, $22 million. 
With respect to gas rates, RRA estimates that about $96 million of the roughly $237 million difference between the $402.2 million rate increase supported by 
CECONY and the $165.4 million increase recommended by staff flows from the staff's lower rate-of-return recommendation. 
Staff-proposed rate base adjustments lower the revenue requirement by $5 million, while suggested disallowances to NOI account for the remaining $136 
million of the gas revenue requirement difference. The major gas NOI adjustments are related to: depreciation, $40 million; municipal operations-interference 
expenses, $25 million; property taxes, $23 million; amortization of regulatory deferrals, $15 million; information technology, $8 million; labor, $5 million; and 
pension and other post-employment benefits costs, $5 million. 
The staff does not support the company's proposed inflation reconciliations adjustments for nonlabor O&M and wages. According to the staff, if the inflation 
proposals were allowed by the PSC, "it would severely lessen the incentives for the Company to work to reduce costs" and "eliminating this incentive would be 
unreasonable during a time in which the whole economy, including ratepayers, are faced with heightened cost pressures." In addition, the staff finds that 
"inflation proposals would significantly reduce the level of risk borne by the Company's shareholders," without an explanation from the company "as how this 
would impact the relative risk of its equity to that of the overall market, and thus, how it would impact its return on equity."
